     Response:    The comment regarding table headings is correct; the

     MW-4S "airport". The lock on MW-16 was broken several months after

Comments Submitted to NJDEP by Geraghty & Miller on behalf of Princeton
Gamma Tech (PGT)
PGT engaged the services of Arent, Fox, Kintner, Plotkin & Kahn
(Arent et al.) to prepare comments on the RI/FS and PRAP. Arent et
al. additionally hired Geraghty & Miller, Inc. (G & M) and Roy F.
Weston, Inc. to present comments.
The comments were numerous and diverse as indicated by their extensive
volume, although three major issues are noted within these comments. These
major issues are as follows:
     Concern was expressed that portions of the RI were not adequately
     performed. Examples of inadequacies included a lack of details in the
     background search, and lack of a soil gas survey.
     PGT noted that other possible source areas exist and expressed
     concern that these areas were not eliminated as potential sources.
    Preference was noted for Alternative #2, which consisted of providing
    water connections, but not flushing the aquifer.
As a response to the first major issue, it must be noted that the background
search, and the scope of the site investigation at these sites are already
relatively extensive.        The presentation of additional background
information, or the recommendation that another one of the myriad of
investigative techniques be utilized does not alter the results of the RI.
Specifically, this information does not detract from the findings that
relatively high concentrations of TCE are present in a location which
could impact the ground water of both RHMW and MTHD.
Regarding the second issue, significant efforts were made to identify and      3
characterize all potential sources within the study area. NJDEP recognizes     ^
the fact that these efforts do not virtually eliminate the possibility that
any given location is a contamination source. To accomplish this task would    o
amount to conducting a major field investigation at every property in the      o
study area, which is not required to meet the objectives of the study.
                                                                               o
                                                                               CD

===== SOURCE PDF FILE PAGE 51 (file index only; use the page number printed in the source text for evidence anchors) =====

                                    -14-


The third issue must be based on the comparison of the benefits of a more
expedient cleanup and the disadvantages of Increased costs. Review of the
costs and cleanup times of the different alternatives presented in the RI/FS
resulted in a marked preference on the part of NJDEP and USEPA for
alternatives utilizing a limited aquifer flushing scheme.
MTHD Contamination:
1.   The estimates of maximum rate erf ground water flow found in the RI
     prove that PGT could not have contaminated the ground water under
     MTHD.    Shallow ground watex' flow from PGT is in a northwest or
     west-northwest direction away from the MTHD. In addition, available
     data indicate that deep ground water flow from PGT would be in the
     southeast direction also away from the MTHD. Further, there is no
     explanation as to why PGT's ECRA water-level analyses were excluded
     from the RI/FS whereas the chemical analyses are included (at Vol. 3,
     Appendix 1).
     Response: Several homes in the MTHD are currently supplied by
     municipal water. Prior to connection to the municipal water supply,
     the homes were supplied by privately owned individual wells.        The
     comment fails to address the Impact of the pumping of the individual
     wells on the ground water flow direction and flow rate. Additionally,
     although the ground water flow component at the PGT facility is
     predicted (by PGT consultants) to be to the northwest, the regional
     ground water flow component is to the northeast as evidenced by
     fracture trace analyses and ground water flow contours as stated in the
     RI/FS report.    The northeast orientation of the primary fracture
     system, coupled with the pumping of all the MTHD individual
     residential wells, could have potentially induced ground water flow
     toward the MTHD greater than what is currently evidenced beneath the
     PGT facility.
     The water level information collected by PGT under the ECRA program
     was not included in the appendix since it was not referenced in the
     RI. This data was not referenced for the following reasons:
     It was preliminary and only part of ongoing PGT activities regarding
     water levels.
     As noted above, it did not present information beyond that presented in
     the regional study of the RI.
     Minor discrepancies were noted in the reported well casing elevations,
     which would have required resolution prior to inclusion in the report.
RHMW Contamination:
2.   PGT could not have contaminated the RHMW for the following reasons:
     a.   Contaminated shallow ground water under PGT flows naturally away       -<
          from the RHMW (G & M Assessments, Figures 1 and 7; pages
          2,11-12);                                                             g

                                                                               o
                                                                               03
                                                                               IV>
                                                                               \1

                                    -15-




     b.   Pumping action does not draw the contaminated water to the RHMW
          (RI at 5-5 and 6-3) and;
     c.   Wells between PGT and the RHMW were not contaminated (RI at
          5-29 Table 5-7).
     Response;     No conclusion has been reached stating that PGT
     contaminated the ground water entering either RHMW or MTHD.       The
     conclusions presented in the RI/FS report note that the property is a
     primary source area for contamination to the sites.       Among other
     factors presented in the report, the results used in forming this
     conclusion Include a water level in Monitor Well MW-7D of 3 to 4 feet
     below that in MW-75, a water level in (MW-7D) and RHMW of 5 to 60
     feet below that in MW-7D, and the presence of ground water TCE
     contamination in PGT's MW-1 in concentrations typically 20 times
     greater than that in RHMW.     The general downward water flow, the
     location of the PGT property upgradient of any contaminated offsite
     location, and the presence of cm-site TCE in concentrations well
     above those found offsite is typical across the entire RHMW/MTHD
     site.
     The presence of TCE in MW-9D, MW-9S and the PGT production well
     only serves to further support the above conclusions, since the only
     Identified upgradient source of contamination to these wells is the
     PGT property.      The variations in shallow well elevations are
     negligibly minor when compared to the differences found both within the
     on-site well pair, and across the site as a whole.
Deficiencies of RI/FS:
3.   Public participation was not timely invited.
     Response: In accordance with the requirements of the Comprehensive
     Environmental Response, Compensation, and Liability Act (CERCLA), and
     the Superfund Amendments Reauthorization Act (SARA), NJDEP conducted an
     extensive community relations program. The major community relations
     activities conducted at the MTHD/RHMW sites to date are outlined
     earlier in this Responsiveness Summary under the Chronology of
     Community Relations Activities.       PGT received a hand-delivered
     informational flyer describing planned RI/FS activities in November
     1985.
4.   Why did NJDEP's Consultant, Woodward-Clyde Consultants (WCC), not

     aerial photographs considered by G & M? What indications of disturbed
     areas did WCC find on the aerial photographs it considered? How did
     its findings influence decisions to sample or not to sample?

===== SOURCE PDF FILE PAGE 53 (file index only; use the page number printed in the source text for evidence anchors) =====

                                      -16-


     Resoonse; The degree to which aerial photographs were used in this
     RI/FS is considered to be appropriate by NJDEP/USEPA. Although aerial
     photos can be a useful supplement to a ground water investigation,
     primary data collection should be relied upon as the principal
     investigative technique. The fact that the 6 & H aerial photo review
     found no significant new information further supports this.

     boring SB-3 is in this location.

     monitor wells and a septic sample.      PGT's request for file access
     should be coordinated through the NJDEP Division of Hazardous Waste
     Management.

23. The number and location of water-level measurement points (monitoring
    wells) that form the basis of the RI at the MTHD is insufficient for
    an assessment of shallow ground water flow conditions in this area.
      Response:   The hydrologic investigation is considered adequate and
      appropriate by NJDEP and USEPA.
24.   The deep monitoring wells are cased in the bedrock over almost the
      entire thickness of the Brunswick Aquifer to the depth the wells were
      drilled. This does not allow for an assessment of ground water flow
      conditions in the deeper portions of the Brunswick Aquifer.        In
      addition, well depths are highly variable (85 to 250 feet), further
      complicating an assessment of deeper flow conditions.
      Response;    The investigative methodology is considered appropriate
      mostly based upon the fact that natural flow is predominately within
      vertical fractures.
25.   Given the VCC pumping test results and their statement that "the
      facility for contaminant migration would be greatest along strike"
      (RI, Page 3-6), it is surprising that the WCC study did not
      concentrate on studying potential sources that lie along formation
      strike in proximity to RHMW.
      Response;    The investigation maintained an effort to investigate
      potential sources of contamination to MTHD/RHMW sites without
      concentrating along the strike.    While the ground water flow is
      greatest along the strike, flow perpendicular to the strike is also
      expected.
26.   The Public Health Assessment (PHA) did not address the No Action and
      Aquifer Isolation alternatives in any detail. The Aquifer Isolation
      Alternative warrants consideration.
      Response; The PHA was based upon a No Action scenario. The Aquifer
      Isolation Alternative has been considered throughout the RI/FS Study.
      The Alternative, however, was not as effective at achieving the site
      objectives as the selected alternative.
27.   How was the "perceived likelihood of finding non-volatlles in a given
      boring" evaluated (RI at p. 4-5)?
      Response;    Selection of samples for analysis was based upon the
      following instructions presented to VCC:
      Hierarchy for deciding which samples are to be sent to the lab are as
      follows (in descending preference).
                  a)   High OVA reading
                  b)   Visible contamination
                  c)   Bottom of Boring (Water-Table)
                  d)   2-4 feet depth

28. The statement (RI at p. 6-7) referring to "the plume" as having
    achieved "steady state" is inaccurate.     The TCE concentrations are
    erratic with time, and no other data are presented to indicate that any
    "plume" has achieved steady state.
     Response: In context, the statement in question refers to a lack of
     significant trends.  Variations in samples at a given location are
     recognized.
IV. REMAINING CONCERNS
Many of the issues addressed in this Responsiveness Summary will continue to
be of concern to the community.         Montgomery Township officials have
requested that they be kept up-to-date on Remedial Design activities,
including a briefing during this phase, and have also requested an
opportunity to provide input into the Design. Honoring of this request
should help to further alleviate some of the above concerns, including those
of noise and air pollution and placement of the pump and treat units.
The concerns regarding the sealing of private wells and connections to the
public water supply system for the MTHD are generally being addressed
under the September 1987 Operable Unit Record of Decision.
The issue of payments for past water line connections in MTHD is
continuing to be addressed by NJDEP and USEPA under applicable laws and
regulations.
Although NJDEP and USEPA have been able to identify a primary source of the
contamination, a responsible party(ies) has not been named.           Should
payment to the communities for past actions be precluded by applicable laws
and regulations, then the question of reimbursement for past costs may
remain unanswered until a responsible party(ies) is identified, at which
time the communities may choose to pursue reimbursement from the responsible
party(ies).

Site Description!
The Montgomery Township Housing Development site consists of 71 residences, each
on approximately one acre, situated on Sycamore Lane, Robin Drive, Oxford Circle
and Cleveland Circle in Montgomery Township. The Rocky Hill Municipal Vellfield
site is a two-acre tract of land located to the south of the Montgomery Township
Housing Development in Rocky Hill Borough.
The major environmental concern at these sites is contamination of the ground
water with trichloroethylene (TCE) ard other pollutants. The source(s) of this
contamination remains unknown. All of the 71 residences have private wells which
draw water x from the Brunswick Shale Formation. Since the discovery of ground
water contamination, 33 homes have been connected to the Elizabethtown Water
Company's public water supply. Some remaining residences in and around the
development continue to draw water from the Brunswick aquifer.
The aquifer also supplies drinking water for approximately 1,000 residents of
Rocky Hill and is used as a source of potable and process water, as well as for
irrigation.   However, two air stripping units presently treat the Pocky Rill
Municipal Veil so that it meets potable water standards.
The remaining public health concern is that some residents may still be using
TCE-contaminated well water. The flow of contaminants in the fractured rock
aquifer' is difficult to predict and future samples may show contamination of
previously uncontaminated wells. Several potential sources of contamination
(industrial and commercial establishments in the area) have been identified. TCE
was also detected in septic tanks of commercial or industrial establishments at 6
of 13 identified potential sources.
                                                                                         3
Background:                                                                              ^
In 1979, the Rocky Hill Municipal Well was closed due to elevated levels of TCE. o
These levels eventually declined and the well was reopened in July, 1981 only to o
be shut down for a second time in January, 1982 when TCE levels again Increased. *"*
                                                                                 o
                                                                                 CO
                                                                                 (*)
                                                                                         00

===== SOURCE PDF FILE PAGE 63 (file index only; use the page number printed in the source text for evidence anchors) =====

                                                 I
                                                     I


During thtsa Interim closure*, vattr was supplied to the Borough by the
Elirabethtown Water Company. Since July, 1983 the well has been operating with
air stripping units.    Concern over the findings in Rocky Hill triggered a
sampling program for the neighboring Montgomery Township Housing Development.
                                                               *

In May, 1983 the New Jersey Department of Environmental Protection (NJDEF) began
Investigations to determine the sources of contamination and the direction of
ground water flow. Cooperative Agreements between NJDEP and the United States
Environmental Protection Agency to perform Remedial Investigations/Feasibility
Studies (RI/FS) were finalised In the fall of 1984.
One RZ/FS is being conducted for both sites due to their close proximity and the
potentially related source(s) of contamination. The study which was initiated in
September 1985 is being conducted by Woodward-Clyde Consultants of Wayne, NJ and
is expected to be completed during the winter of 1986.
Of 99 New Jersey sites on the National Priorities List (Superfund), the
Montgomery Township Housing Development and Rocky Hill Municipal Wellfield are
ranked 65th and 67th respectively.

                      REMEDIAL INVESTIGATIOK/FEASIBILITY STUDY
    Montgomery Township Housing Development/Rocky Hill Municipal Vellfield
Objectives
 "Determine types and concentration of hazardous substances present.
 "Define extent of contamination and movement of contaminants.
 "Try to Identify sources of the contamination.
 'Develop and evaluate remedial alternatives to determine the most
   environmentally-sound and cost-effective remedial action measure.
 "Projected completion: winter of 1986.
Pre-investigation Activities
 "Develop Project Schedule, Health and Safety Plan, Field Sampling Plan, and
  Quality Assurance Project Management Plan.
 "Conduct background investigation including: A review of available information
  regarding the sites; a site reconnaissance; and an inventory of existing
  drinking water wells within a one-mile radius of the sites.
 "Perform an analysis of surface geology and geophysical investigation.
 "Identify appropriate locations for monitoring wells.
Site Investigations
 "Install monitoring wells and drill soil borings.
 "Collect water, soil and septic tank samples for chemical analysis.
 "Monitor air quality during site investigations.
 "Define subsurface migration pathvay(s).
 "Assess aquifer characteristics and drinking water quality.
 "Identify the most probable sources of contamination.
 "Identify all hazardous substances.
Remedial Response
 "Develop remedial response objectives and identify appropriate remedial
  alternatives.
 "Determine and recommend level of cleanup required at the sites and/or vicinity.
Treatability Studies
 "Conduct all necessary laboratory sample analyses.
 "Develop and conduct a traatability/compatibility study program to provide data
   for remedial alternatives evaluation.
 "Use data from the laboratory programs during selection of appropriate remedial
  measures.

                           Glossary of Terms

Administrative Consent Order (AGO); A binding legal document between a
government agency and a responsible party. It is issued by the
government in the form of an order that specifies site mitigation
activities to be undertaken by the responsible party.•
Contract; ' The legal agreement that outlines federal and state
government responsibilities at USEPA-lead sites on the National
Priorities List (Superfund sites) as authorized by the Comprehensive
Environmental Response* Compensation and Liability Act (CERCLA).
Cooperative Agreement; An agreement whereby USEPA transfers funds and
other resources to a state for the accomplishment of certain remedial
activities at sites on the Kational Priorities List (Superfund sites)
as authorized by the Comprehensive Environmental Response, Compensation
and Liability Act (CERCLA).
Engineering Design (Remedial Design); Following a feasibility study,
an engineering design is executed to translate the selected remedy in
accordance with engineering criteria in a bid package, enabling
implementation of the site remedy.
Focused Feasibility Study (FFS); A limited feasibility study which is
performed on a certain aspect of site remediation and/or when more than
one remedial measure is considered technically viable for the immediate
control of a threat
Immediate Removal Actions (IRAs); Actions taken to prevent or mitigate
immediate and significant risk to human life, health or to the
environment.
Initial Remedial Measures (IRMs); Actions that can be taken quickly to
limit exposure or threat of exposure to a significant health or
environmental hazard at sites where planning for remedial actions is
underway. .
Monitoring Well; A well installed under strict design specifications
that, when sampled, will reveal hydrogeloglc data at its point of
installation.     Monitoring wells are installed at predetermined
locations, usually in groups, to gain knowledge of site conditions
including: extent and type of ground water contamination, soil types,
depth to ground water and direction of ground water flow.
Kational Contingency Plan (KCP)t    The basic policy directive for
federal response actions under the Comprehensive Environmental
Response, Compensation and Liability Act (CERCLA). It sets forth the
Hazard Ranking System and procedures and standards for responding to
release* of hazardous substances, pollutants, and contaminants. The       3
NCP is a regulation subject to regular revision.                          ^
National Priorities List (KPL);  A list of the highest priority           o
releases or potential releases of hazardous substances, based upon        o

                                                                          o
                                                                          o>

===== SOURCE PDF FILE PAGE 67 (file index only; use the page number printed in the source text for evidence anchors) =====

Statt and U.S. Environotntal Protection Agency (USEPA) Regional
•ubmiaaiona of candidate aitea and the criteria and methodology
contained in the Hazard Ranking System (HRS), for the purpose of
allocating funds for remedial response under the Comprehensive
Environmental Response, Compensation and Liability • Act (CERCLA).
Published by the USEPA, the NPL is updated periodically. Sites on the
NPL are commonly called Suparfund sites.
NJPEP: New Jersey Department of Environmental Protection.
NJDEP'e Management Plan for Hazardous Waste Site Cleanupa;   The    New
Jersey plan used to develop a work schedule and a systematic approach
to remedial action at hazardous waste sites and discharges of hazardous
materials which posa a threat to public health or the environment.
Remedial Action; (e.g., Removal/Treatment/Construction) The physical
action consistent with the selected remedy for a release or threatened
release of a hazardoua substance into the environment. The term
includes, but is not limited to such action* as removal, storage,
confinement, protection using dikes, tranches, ditches, slurry walls,
clay cover, neutralization, cleanup of releaaed hazardous substances or
contaminated materials, recycling or reuse, diversion, destruction,
segregation of reactive wastes, dredging or excavationa, repair or
replacement of leaking containers, collection of leachate and runoff,
on-site or off-site treatment or incineration, provision of alternate
water supplies, snd monitoring required to sssure that such actions
protect public health and the environment.
Remedial Investigation/Feasibility Study (RI/FS);  The     Remedial
Investigation (RI) portion of a RI/FS in remedial planning involvea a
physical and other investigation to gather the data necessary to
determine the nature and extent of problems at the site; establish
remedial response criteria for the site; and identify technical and
cost analyses of the alternatives. The Feasibility Study (FS) portion
of a RX/FS in remedial planning involvea a study to evaluate
alternative remedial actions from a technical, environmental, and cost
perspective; recommend the most effective remedy for adequate
protection of human health and the environment; and prepare a
conceptual design, cost estimates for budgetary purposes, and a
preliminary implementation schedule for that action.
Responsible Party; Any person who has discharged a hazardous substance
or is In any way responsible for any hazardous substance which the
NJDEP has removed or is removing pursuant to the New Jersey Spill
Compensation and Control Act and/or the Comprehensive Environmental
Response, Compensation and Liability Act (CERCLA).
Spill Compensation Fund: The Spill Compensation Fund was created in
1976 with enactment of the Spill Compensation and Control Act and
became effective on April 1, 1977. It provides compensation to
qualified individuate and businesses that have suffered damages aa a
result of a discharge of hazardous substances.                             o
                                                                           o
USEPA: United States Environmental Protection Agency.                     ""

Sits Description:
The Montgoaary Township Bousing Development (MTHD) Suparfund aita
encompasses approximately 72 acres to the east of U.S. Routs 206 and north
of H.J. Route 518. The development consists of 71 private homes with six
additional homes included in the surrounding area. The Rocky Bill Municipal
Wellfield (RHKW) Superfund site is a two-acre tract of land located to the
aast of Route 206 and aouth of Route 518 in the Borough of Rocky Bill.
Drinking water for the MTHD is supplied by private residential veils and by
the Elicabethtown Water Company. Of the 77 residences* roughly one-half
have been connected to the Elizabethtovn system while the remaining
residents continue to use private wells. Rocky Bill Borough*a public water
system is supplied by the municipal wellfield.       An air stripping system
presently treata the vatar so that it meets drinking wster standards.
Background:
In 1979* the Rocky Bill municipal well was closed due to alavatad levels of
trichloroethene (TCE) and Elizabethtovn Vatar Company provided a temporary
alternative water supply. Sines July 1983, the Borough'a water supply has
been treated by the air stripping aystam. Concern over the Rocky Bill
findings triggered a subsequent sampling program for the neighboring MTHD.
In March 1981, Elizabathtown Water Company water linaa were Installed in
MTHD and residents were advised to connect to these lines*

Remedial Action:     (e.g.* Removal/Treatment/Constructioa)    The physical
action consistent vith the selected remedy for a release or threatened
release of a hatardoua substance into the environment. The term includes,
but ia mot limited to auch actions as removal, storsge, confinement,

containers, collection of leaehate and runoff, en-site or «ff-site treatment
or incineratioa, proviaioa of alternate vater supplies, and monitoring
required to assure that such actions protect public health and the
environment.
Reaediel   Investigation/Feasibility  Study   (RI/FS)t      The   Remedial
Investigation(RI)portion of a RI/FSia remedial planning involves a
physical and other investigation to gather the data necessary to determine
the mature and extent of problems at the aite; establish remedial response
criteria for the sits; and identify technical and cost analyses of the —
alternatives. The Feasibility Study (FS) portion of a RI/FS ia remedial
planning involves a study to svaluate alternative remedial aetioas from a
technical, environmental, and cost perspective; recommend the most effective '
remedy for adequate protection of human health and the environment; and
prepare a conceptual design, cost estimates for budgetary purposes, and a
preliminary implementation schedule for that action.

at

As part of the federal/state program of cleanup at hazardous waste sites, a Community Relations
Program la conducted to receive local Input and to advise local residents and officials about
the planned remedial actions at major stages of the cleanup. Local briefings and meetings are
conducted with elected officials and residents and generally take place at:

  v1) The commencement of a remedial Investigation/feasibility study
      so that local concerns can be addressed early In the process.

   2) The completion of a feasibility study to discuss the alternative
      courses of remedial action. There la a 21-day comment period
      on the alternatives during which the feasibility study la available
      In local repositories.

   3) The commencement of the treatment/construction/removal
      stage to advise of the expected physical remedial action.
                                                                                                         o
   4) The completion of the remedial action.                                                             -<
                                                                                                     o
                                                                                                     o
In addition to the activities outlined above, there is generally ongoing communication with local
officials and residents as required. Depending upon whether the New Jersey Department of
Environmental Protection (DEP) or the United States Environmental Protection Agency (EPA)           o
Is the lead agency In remedial action at a alte. community relations activities are conducted by    0>
the relevant State or Federal agency.
In New Jersey, the DEP Community Relations Program la directed by Grace Singer. Chief, Bureai
of Community Relations (609) 984-3081. At Region II. EPA. the Community Relations Coordinator
Is Lillian Johnson. (212) 264-2515.

                                    Re: Princeton Gamma-Tech. Inc.
   Dear Neal:
           Our initial review of PGT data in Volume 3 of the RI/FS discloses the
   following two interesting and important errors:
                  Appendix B, Septic Tank Analyses, Trichlorethene (TCE), lists an 11/19/79
                  sampling date and an analysis of 8900 (ppb).
       *\
                  Appendix B, Septic Tank Analyses, Trichlorethene (TCE), lists no DEP
                  sampling on 1/17/80.
   These are both erroneous. DEP did sample PGTs septic tank on 11/19/79, as
   reflected in the Toluene and Benzene analyses, as well as the V.O. Scan, for that
   date in Volume 3, Appendix B, Septic Tank Analyses. However, DEP's analysis found
   no TCE. (See the attached copy of DEP's sample analysis sheet for 11/19/79.] DEP
   sampled PGTs septic tank a second time on 1/17/80 and, this time, reported an
   analysis of 8900 ppb of TCE. The only sampling data shown for that date (1/17/80)
   in all of Volume 3 is under the heading "V.O. Scan" and consists of "ND+" in the
   PGT column. "ND+" is defined as "not detected except for compounds indicated
   below" but no compounds are so indicated.
                                                                     •
            The errors are interesting because they raise the possibility that Woodward-
   Clyde Consultants misplaced a whole set of DEP sampling results from 1/17/80. They -
   certainly misplaced the results of the PGT sample. Now it seems implausible that
   DEP, having initially sampled some dozen or so septic tanks in November 1979 at the
   outset of its investigation (and doing so again, by another full round of sampling in 3
   February 1980) would, in January 1980 re-sample only one of those dozen or. so ^ »
   septic tanks. In all likelihood. WWC simply mislaid the other results of sampling all      :
   the other septic tanks on that day.             .                                     0
                                                                                         o
            Accordingly, PGT requests that DEP locate and share with us the other ^
   results of 1/17/80 samplings of all the septic tanks so that we may all learn from Q
    them.                                 i.                                              co
                                                                                          <^
                                                                                          *-+
            Telephone: (202) R57-6000 CaWt: ARFOX Tartx: WU 892672 ITT 440266 Telecopier (202) 6576395

===== SOURCE PDF FILE PAGE 86 (file index only; use the page number printed in the source text for evidence anchors) =====

Arent, Fox, Kinmer, Plotkin &. Kahn
Neal Brody. Esq.
April 21, 1988
Page 2


         The errors are important because WWC seems to have concluded that —
many years before the isolated spill in 1980 (which appears to have been remediated
before any escape to the septic leach field, as reflected in DBF's own analyses of its
soil borings in the leach field Isee Volume 3, Appendix B, Soil Analyses]) — there
was a large prior spill event into the septic system at the PGT property. If DEP's
discovery of 8900 ppb of TCE had actually taken place on the first round of
sampling, on 11/17/79, it might conceivably have led WWC to its conclusions (Volume
2, pages 2-7) "that this (the PGT property] was the primary source location of TCE
contamination" and that:
              While an earlier septic tank sample identified the presence of
         TCE, recent samples found only residual TCE in the septic tank and
         none in the soil. This indicates the probability that some past
         discharge was the source of contamination found in the shallow •
         regions of the aquifer.
If these conclusions are based on the sampling of PGTs septic tank, we believe that
WWC would have had to have reached a different interpretation had it realized that
DEP's first sampling of that septic tank revealed no TCE •• quite inconsistent with a
past discharge (which should have left some residue in the septic tank in November
1979).

        As you will recall, WWC elsewhere concluded that the original contamination
must date back 25 years or more — that is, to 1963 or earlier. [See Volume 1,
pages 6-8.] Apparently, although not clearly stated. WWC has conceived the
erroneous impression that 8900 ppb remained in the septic tank in November 1979
from a past spill that had occurred at least 16 years before that 1979 sampling!
Correction of WWC's erroneous impression, and a concomitant reassessment of its
conclusions, is of the highest importance to correctly solving the issues.
        Accordingly, PGT requests that the DEP verify the errors and have WWC
supply a corrected substitute page or pages in Volume 3, Appendix B, Septic Tank
Analyses. Thereafter, PGT requests that DEP ask WWC to reassess their conclusions.
         Specifically, we request the following:
         1.   Please correct the errors and omissions:
              a.   On the TCE line dated 11/19/79, please delete "8900" and substitute
                   "•".
              b.   Have all the other 11/19/79 lines reviewed as to all sites to
                   determine whether other errors were made.

                                                                                         0>

                 c.   Add a TCE line dated 1/17/80 and insert the correct amounts for
                      PGT and for any other sampled septic tanks.
                 d.   Determine whether, in fact, no other site but PGTs was sampled
                      on 1/17/80. If other sites were in fact sampled, please supply the
                      analytical data for such sites.
          2. If no other site save PGTs was in fact sampled on 1/17/80 (as Appendix
    B now indicates), please advise us of the reason why.
           3. Please request WWC to reassess their conclusions in light of the fact
    that DEP found no TCE in the PGT property septic tank on 11/17/79.

INTRODUCTION

Background

     A Princeton Gamma Tech (P6T) facility located on Route 518
just east of the intersection with Route 206 in Somerset County,
New Jersey has been identified as a likely primary source of
trichloroethene (TCE) contamination of groundwater for the Rocky
Hill Municipal Well (RKMW) and the Montgomery Township Housing
Development (KTHD). This identification is 'the result of a
Remedial Investigation/Feasibility Study (RI/FS) that was
performed.

    The purpose of this report is to evaluate the RI/FS by
examining the scientific and professional rationale on which it
is based, and the validity of identifying PGT as the source of
TCE contamination.

Findings

     Based on an understanding of scientific principles, accepted
professional practices and the hydrogeologic characteristics of
the site, the RI/FS appears significantly flawed in a number of
areas. These flaws bring into question the validity of
identifying PGT as the source of TCE contamination.

METHODS

Documents Reviewed

     The following   documents have been     reviewed   for   this
evaluations

     (a) Remedial Investigation/Feasibility Study for Montgomery
         Township Rousing Development/Rocky Rill Municipal 3
         Wellfield Site (April, 1988) Volume Is Remedial "
         Investigation, prepared by Woodward-Clyde Consultants, g
         Wayne, New Jersey.                                      ^
                                                                     o
                                                                     00

===== SOURCE PDF FILE PAGE 94 (file index only; use the page number printed in the source text for evidence anchors) =====

     (b) Remedial Investigation/Feasibility Study for Montgomery
         Township Housing Development/Rocky Hill Municipal
         Wellfield Site (March, 1988) Volume 2: Feasibility
         Study, prepared by Woodward-Clyde Consultants, Wayne,
           Mew Jersey.

     (e) Remedial Investigation/Feasibility Study for Montgomery
         Township Housing Development/Rocky Hill Municipal
         Wellfield Site (April, 1988) Volume 3:      Appendices,
           prepared by Woodward-Clyde Consultants, Wayne, New
           Jersey.

     Supplemental information was obtained from the ECRA file
(Case ft85558) for the PGT site from the New Jersey Department of
Environmental Protection (NJDEP).

     All   documents     were   reviewed   for    appropriate       problem
definition, accuracy in execution of methods, completeness,
documentation of cause-and-effect relationships and consistency
in interpretation. This review revealed a number of perceived
flaws that are individually described below.

Site Visits

     Messrs. John F. Bolakas and Emil Onuschak, senior
hydrogeologists with Groundwater Technology, Inc., visited and
photographed the site on 20 and 24 May 1988 for familiarization
and to clarify field relationships.

CONCEPTUAL ERRORS
                                                              *

     Perceived conceptual error* are those that relate to
inappropriate or incomplete problem definition, selective use of
data and failure to establish cause-and-effect relationships.


                                                 MGY   001   0870

===== SOURCE PDF FILE PAGE 95 (file index only; use the page number printed in the source text for evidence anchors) =====

Inventory of Potential Sources

     The remedial investigation names 13 potential sources of
contamination, which are from 500 feet to 4000 feet away from
RHMW |2.    Six of these potential sources are in the northeast
quadrant of the intersection of Routes 206 and 518.

     The remedial investigation fails to identify or discuss the
Prineeton North Shopping Center, which is at closest approach 150
feet west of RHMW 12 and includes a six-bay Goodyear auto service
store (Prineeton Tire), a print shop offering offset printing and
copying services (Triangle Reproduction) and a dry cleaners (Mrs.
B's Dry Cleaning and Laundry) offering "cleaning done on
premises." An underground waste oil tank is located in front of
the auto service area at the Goodyear auto service store
approximately 150 feet southwest of RHMW |2. This potential
source, the age of the tank and the tank integrity are not
addressed in the RI. Since service garages also commonly use
waste oil tanks for a variety of mixed wastes, including
chlorinated degreasing solvents, this waste oil tank is a prime
candidate for further investigation.

     Mrs. B's Dry Cleaning and Laundry is the second-closest
known potential source of contamination to RHMW |2, located
approximately 200 feet southwest of the well. Zt is not known
whether the Goodyear facility or Mrs. B's Dry Cleaning and
Laundry have ever been connected to septic tank disposal systems.
This entire shopping center merits further close attention.

     On the west side of Route 206 in the same general area as
Prineeton Volkswagen is a second Prineeton Gamma Tech facility.

Limitations         .

     The RI contains the stated assumption that the Brunswick
Formation acts as a porous medium equivalent and meets the
classical definitions of unconfined and semiconfined aquifers.
Without citing specific examples, it is simply Asserted that
"...evaluation of cores, field observation of water levels and
examination of the slope of curves from the pumping test imply
that the aquifer zones approximate a porous medium equivalent"
(page 7-3). This is followed by a general statement that as a
larger and larger areal extent of aquifer is considered, "...and
horizontal flow predominates'-over vertical flow, the system comes
closer to meeting the porous medium limitations. Thus, for the
site as a whole, analytical methods based on porous medium flow
can be appropriate" (page 7-4).

     In reality, although the boring logs indicate that the
Brunswick Formation is extensively fractured in the area, the
quantitative measure of fracture orientations presented in Figure
5-2 shows that the aquifer is very heterogeneous. More fractures
occur-in the northeast-southwest direction than in any other.

     Furthermore, the field observation of water levels during
pumping (Figure 5-5) shows a pronounced elliptical area of
influence rather than the circular, or nearly circular, area that
would be expected if the aquifer was homogeneous (a "porous
medium equivalent").

     And finally, the drawdown curves generated during the
pumping test present an assortment of slopes, curves and data
points that are vulnerable to selective interpretation, to say ~
the least.                                                       is  o
                                                                     <
     In one case, two drawdown curves are presented for MW-11D
apparently showing, via high correlation coefficients, that the      o
aquifer there is both fractured (drawdown plotted against square
                                                                     o
                                                                     00
                                                                     Nf

===== SOURCE PDF FILE PAGE 97 (file index only; use the page number printed in the source text for evidence anchors) =====

                                     I
foot of elapsed tine, correlation coefficient * 0.99) and
non-fractured (drawdown plotted against elapsed tine, correlation
coefficient * 1.00) at the sane tine.

     The statement of limitations in the HI is merely a general
disclaimer and several unsubstantiated assertions that the
methods used are correct, despite the existence of data
indicating otherwise.

PROCEDURAL ERRORS

     Procedural errors include reaching conclusions unsupported
by qualified data (anecdotal information), reaching conclusions
inconsistent with qualified data (cause-and-effect), mistakes in
data acquisition (poor monitoring well design, poor QA/QC) and
mistakes in data handling (selective use of data, mistaken use of
nomenclature,    inconsistent   interpretations,    typographical _
errors).

RI/FS Timetable

     The date on the Feasibility Study Volume 2 (March 1988)
precedes the date on the Remedial Investigation Volume 1 (April
1988). This suggests that identification of the source area is
biased since the Remedial Investigation apparently was completed
after the Feasibility Study.

Anecdotal Information

     Unsubstantiated anecdotal information regarding TCE usage
appears to be extensively relied upon to draw conclusions
regarding the source area. A reference to P6T on page 3-12
states "The lab had reportedly used (1*2 gallons per month) in
the past". This statement does not reference the source of this
information, nor indicate whether the 1-2 gallons was disposed
of properly, reused or recycled. The implication of TCE being

Well Installation and Sampling     \
                            •


     Ten of the thirty wells installed for the RI could not be
sampled during the first round because they were dry. Zt appears
that a significant number of wells were not adequately designed
for the scope of the project.

     MW-4S and MW-5S were dry during both rounds of sampling.
Based on the obvious purpose of sampling water from the upper
xone of the water table, these wells were not adequately designed
and/or .correctly installed to meet project objectives. A similar
assumption can be drawn regarding MW-6S which was dry during the
first round of sampling and had insufficient water during the
second round of sampling to analyze for volatile organic
compounds.

     MW-10S was not sampled during either first or second round
of the RI sampling program. During the first round, the well was
not sampled because it contained no water. Zt is not clear from
the chart on page 5-28 of the RI why the well was not sampled
during the second round.
                                        i              •

     Zn addition, monitoring wells MW-15S, KW-16, MW-17, MW-l
and MW-19 were not sampled during the first round of sampling
Zt is not clear if these wells were not sampled due to lack oi

===== SOURCE PDF FILE PAGE 100 (file index only; use the page number printed in the source text for evidence anchors) =====

water. MW-16 in particular would have provided particularly
valuable data for sampling round 1 because it is between the MW-4
and MW-7 well pairs.

     Groundwater temperatures in this part of the country
characteristically range from 10° to 12.7° C .(50° - 55° F).
Temperatures of groundwater samples taken for the two rounds of
sampling ranged from 5° C (41° F) to 23.0 ° C (73.4° F). This
excessively wide range suggests that either the thermometer used
nay have been faulty, not enough time was allowed for the
thermometer to reach equilibrium, or that the wells were
inadequately purged to provide a representative sample. The data
presented on page 5-28 of the RI does not reflect ranges expected
to assure an adequate QA/QC protocol during sampling. At a
minimum, the significant temperature range should have been
discussed and justified.

Pump Test

     The absence of significant drawdown in the shallow wells
indicates that the 6 -hour pump test was not of sufficient
duration since the well logs indicate that the deep/shallow pairs
actually monitor the same groundwater system.

     The elevations of the base of the shallow monitoring zone
and the top of the deep monitoring zone for each pair of
monitoring wells are listed in Table 1. With the exception of
well pair II, the shallow zone monitored by each well pair ends
at the same elevation where the deep monitoring zone begins.
Given the extensively fractured nature of the aquifer, which is
described in the boring logs, it must be assumed that the shallow
and deep zones are in hydraulic communication at each pair of
monitoring wells and that any data represented as "shallow*
versus "deep" must be interpreted carefully.

     A 6-hour aquifer test cannot yield sufficient data for a
detailed analysis of aquifer characteristics. Because the
calculation of transmissivity (T) is dependent on the slope of
the drawdown curve, correct choice of slope is essential to any
interpretation. Six hours of pumping is not sufficient to see if
more than one slope is present.

      In the case of the pumping well, early data (0-100 minutes)
is "of ten affected by casing storage. Yet the RZ uses the early
data to calculate a transmissivity value for the aquifer. Early
data «50 minutes) was also used to calculate T for wells MW-9D _
and MW-10D but later data (50 minutes - 360 minutes) was used for
wells KW-11D, KW-15D and MW-7D. It appears that the choice of 2
data points was based solely on those points falling in a -<
straight line, without consideration of the hydrologic 0
characteristics which they represent.                             ^
                                                                    o
                                                                    0>
                                                                    NS
                                  11

===== SOURCE PDF FILE PAGE 102 (file index only; use the page number printed in the source text for evidence anchors) =====

     A drastic increase in drawdown occurred at 110 minutes in
all wells, yet pumping did not continue past 360 minutes to
monitor -any additional changes. This steepening of drawdown
curve could represent a sudden dewatering of fractures or
encroachment on an impervious boundary. Additional pumping and
monitoring may have yielded further data to determine what this
change represents.

     The pump test data shows that approximately 0.5 foot of
drawdown was observed at POT, which is 1,000 feet away from RHMW
12 and across structural trends, while at Princeton Airport
approximately 4.5 feet of drawdown was observed 2,000 feet away
along structural trends.       These data clearly show that
groundwater flow is anisotropic and is structurally controlled,
which is consistent with the fracture data summarized in the RI
(Figure 5-2). Based on these data, groundwater (and any
accompanying contamination) should be expected to move toward
RHMW |2 from a greater distance in a northeast-southeast
direction. Hydrogeologic characteristics of the area should
therefore inhibit (but do not necessarily prevent) the movement
of groundwater
       \
               flow from PGT to RHMW 12.

Nature and Quality of Groundwater Data

     With the exception of well pair 11, the shallow zone
monitored by each well pair ends at the same elevation where the
deep monitoring zone begins. Given the extensively fractured
nature of the aquifer, which is described in the boring logs, it
must be assumed that the shallow and deep cones are in hydraulic
communication at each pair of monitoring wells, offering man-made
pathways for deep infiltration of shallow contaminants, driven by
existing vertical gradients (Table 5-1). Furthermore, the
thickness of aquifer opened in almost every deep monitoring well
is quite large. Thus, groundwater samples from these monitoring
wells must be regarded as composite samples with possible
man-made impacts, and water quality data cannot be interpreted
with reference to its alleged vertical position.
                                \
                                12
                                         MGY   001   0878

===== SOURCE PDF FILE PAGE 103 (file index only; use the page number printed in the source text for evidence anchors) =====

     Round two sampling took approximately seven weeks to
complete (Table 5-6, Volume 1, page 5-28). This is too long.
There is no assurance that subsurface conditions at the beginning
of the sampling period were the same as at the end.    Therefore,
data from round two may not be internally consistent and
comparable.

Groundwater Divide

     The shallow and deep tone groundwater maps, conclusions of
potential sources and conclusion on the groundwater divide are
based on one round of liquid level data from wells with "assumed
static conditions". The RZ states (page 5-3) regarding liquid
level data that "These measurements were made just after RHMW ft 2
had been shut off for 14 hours, allowing water levels to recover
to their static or near-static conditions".

     Based on standard accepted pumping test criteria of 72 hours *
drawdown and 48 hours of recovery, a 14-hour recovery period is
considered insufficient time for complete recovery of a pumping
well prior to starting the actual pump test. Conclusions drawn
from the RHMW |2 pump test data should be considered suspect.

     Due to the importance of conclusions regarding groundwater
flow, and an inferred groundwater divide on site, accurate static
conditions and effects of RHMW 12 pumping are critical. The RZ
does not address the likely effect the intermittent pumping has
on the movement of the inferred groundwater divide. Due to the
relatively flat piezometric surface, intermittent pumping could
easily affect location of the groundwater divide.

     As far back as 31 October 1987,        there were* concerns
regarding actual direction of groundwater flow. Zn J.M. Sorge,
Znc. (JMS) 31 October 1987 ECRA Sampling Plan for Routes 206 and ?
518 facilities, Princeton Gamma Tech, Znc. page 15 - 16 states:

Analytical Data

   • The HI reports that MW-7S and MW-7D contained the highest
concentrations of TCE (650 ug/1) found during the investigation
and therefore must represent the source area. This is a
misrepresentation and misinterpretation of the data since the
second round of data reveals MW-4D contained 630 ug/1 TCE (the
highest level during the second round of sampling), and only a 20
ppb difference noted between the highest level throughout -the
study in 2 rounds of sampling. It is an inappropriate conclusion
that a 20 ppb difference for a selected compound (between MW-7
and MW-4) be used to justify* that the area around KW-7S i and MW-7D
as the source area, based on two rounds of sampling approximately
8 months apart. The inherent variability |n sampling groundwater
contaminants makes it difficult to draw firm conclusions from
small changes in concentrations. Take, for example, the
following data for TCE levels measured in monitoring wells at an
industrial facility (Brown et. al., 1985). (Ref. "Enhanced"
Bioreclamation: Designing a Complete Solution to Groundwater
Problems'1, R.A. Brown et. al., VPCF Industrial Wastes Symposia,
October 8, 1985, Kansas City, MO). The procedure used was to
purge three well volumes and then take four successive samples,
each by withdrawing a fresh bailer-full. Two adjacent wells,
each located within the defined plume area, were sampled. The
data shows the inherent variability in sampling a single well and
in sampling adjacent wells.

2nd Sample              1670                   5,210
3rd Sample              1880                  13,000 *

===== SOURCE PDF FILE PAGE 106 (file index only; use the page number printed in the source text for evidence anchors) =====

     Several observations about the individual wells should be
noted. First the levels of TCE vary randomly within a sampling
•vent. Second, the spread in the data can be greater than the
average concentration. Third, the standard deviation can be
significant enough so that even widely disparate data points are
equally valid. Applying the "2.5d" test for outliers to the well
data would exclude the 780 value for well II (780 > (2.5 x 123))
but not the 5210 value for well 12 (5210 < (2.5 x 4711)). Because
of the variability inherent in sampling, the values from the two
wells should be more aptly expressed ast

          Well ffl 1460 + 478 (982 to 1938)
          Well 12 16,352 + 8,958 (7,394 to 25,310)

     The random variability in values at a single well make it
difficult to predict trends between wells. If one takes the
ratio of the values between samples taken in the two wells there
is a tremendous ranget

          Sample I                        Ratio
     Well il     Well 12             Well 12/Well il
      '1            1                     15.36
       2            2                      3.11
       3            3                      6.91
       4            4                     30.76
       low         high                   30.76
      high         low                     2  . 77

  . Obviously a ratio of 30 would have a greater significance
than a ratio of 2.8. Yet both ratios would be equally likely if
only one sample were taken from each well and compared. A valid
comparison of levels can only be made with consecutive * replicate
campling. Zf an individual sample is used, the only legitimate
conclusion that can be made is that well 12 has more TCE than
does well 11.

     Due to the inherent impurities and composition variations,
chlorinated solvent analysis of trends should have, at a minimum,
been based on the total chlorinated volatile organic compounds,
including tentatively identified compounds. Zf the RI had
reviewed total chlorinated volatile organic compounds, of first
and second sampling rounds, the data would indicate that MW-7S
contained a study high value of 717 ppb, MW-4D a study high value
of 714 ppb and MW-7D a study high value of 712 ppb for total
chlorinated volatile organic compounds. These values for total
chlorinated volatile organic compounds would indicate a 3 ppb
difference between MW-7S and MW-4D. Trend analysis is therefore
also subjective based on parameters chosen for analysis.

     The highest level of any compound noted in groundwater was
1,2-dichloroethene not trichloroethene and is within the ECRA
study area. On 21 January 1988, MW-4 contained 15,000 ppb of
1,2-dichloroethene and 540 ppb trichloroethene. This anomaly in
analytical results is not addressed, based on the trend analysis
presented within the RI.

     The review of the RZ groundwater analytical results for
domestic, industrial and monitoring wells suggest the TCE should
not be used as a single tracer. The inability to provide any
statistically valid trend may suggest that the chlorinated
compounds noted are highly variable and related to multiple
sources .

     The ECRA data presented suggests that if the P6T facility is
at 'the uppermost end of the plume, then the ECRA wells MW-3 and
MW-4 should not be elevated in concentration if they are on the
hydraulically upgradient side of the facility and the site is
within the influence of RHMW |2. As noted above, the* elevated
level of 1,2-dichloroethene relative to trichloroethene suggestr
that there are other sources upgradient of POT.

     Review of the data from the first and second rounds of
sampling were used in the RI to conclude that the PGT property
is the source area. This conclusion is in contradiction with the
data. In addition, the absence of critical data points due to
dry wells MW-4S, MW-5S, MW-6S, MW-9S, MW-10S, MW-15S and absence
of samples at MW-16, MW-17, HW-18, MW-19 and 1377-P result in
sampling round one data being inconclusive to determine a source.
The second round of sampling likely represents a more valid data
base (though not necessary internally consistent due to the
length of time taken to acquire the samples) for interpretation
since there are more data points in critical areas, specifically
MW-9S, MW-10S, MW-16, MW-17,'MW-18, 1377-F and Village Shopper.

     Based on the reasoning applied in the RI, the data from the
second round of sampling (page 5-29 of the HZ) indicates that
highest TCE levels were present around the former Polycell
facility in MW-4D (630 ppb, 590 ppb) and that concentrations
decreased in all directions from around the former Polycell
location. In particular, TCE levels decreased towards PGT, the
RHMW 12 and the KTHD.    MW-17 contained 100 ppb of TCE, MW-7D
contained 180 ppb of TCE, MW-7S contained 160 ppb of TCE, MW-9D
contained 3.75 ppb of TCE and KW-9S contained 6 ppb of TCE.
MW-3D, which is northeast of MW-4 pair and towards the MTDH,
contained 160 ppb of TCE. TCE levels also diminished from well
MW-4D (630 ppb, 590 ppb) towards 1377 Route 206 (150 ppb). By
the same reasoning used in the RI, it could be concluded that the
area around the former Polycell facility and the MW-4 pair are
the source area since concentrations diminish in all directions
from this point, including toward the RHMW, MTHD and PGT.

     During a visit to the site, MW-16 located in the Montgomery
Shopping Center parking lot, was found unsecured by a lock.
NJDEP regulations require locks on all wells. The locks promote
sample integrity, discourage tampering and accidental groundwater
contamination. The absence of a lock, and the integrity of data,
at this location is of particular concern since it is between
monitoring wells MW-7S, MW-7D, and MW-4S, MW-4D, which have had

MW-16 has not.

CONCLUSIONS

     Zn conclusion the review of the RI indicates numerous
shortcomings, inconsistencies, and the absence of sufficient data
to conclude a source for the regional chlorinated groundwater
contamination. Zt should be concluded that before a feasibility
option is chosen that the potential source areas be adequately
defined. Potential responsible parties should only be designated
after adequate delineation and critical failures in the RI's data
base, findings, and conclusions resolved.

             This is to be considered repeat and additional commentary
       related to the May 12, 1988 RIF public meeting and the draft
       Remedial Investigation/Feasibility Study Report and the pro-
       posed Remedial Action Plan discussed that evening.
             The Montgomery Township Board of Health wants it on record
       that it prefers alternate 16, pump/carbon absorption, over alter-
       nate 17, pump/air stripping, for the following reasons.
              1. We do not want to trade off a groundwater contamination
                 problem for an air pollution problem. We are not con-
                 vinced that taking the chemical from the water and dis-
                 persing it into our air will not degrade the air. We
                 prefer chemical removal from our environment over chem-
                 ical dilution into our environment.
              2. We do want to be involved as a municipality with the
                 location and design processes of the selected alterna-
                 tive early on, and we do request public input into de-
                 sign decisions.
              3. If air stipping turns out to be the selected alternative
                 we have concerns in the following areas which we would-
                 like addressed.
                  a. We want assurances with rationale that the air strip-
                     ping units will not be noise nuisances.
                  b. We want assurances with rationale that odors from the
                     air stripping process will not be detectible.

                                      INTRODUCTION

                   Prineeton Gamma-Tech, Inc. ("PGT") appreciatea the
           opportunity afforded by the New Jersey Department of
           Environmental Protection ("DEP") to comment on the Proposed
           Remedial Action Plan ("PRAP") and the underlying Remedial
           investigation/Feasibility Study (-RI/FS") for National
           Priority List Superfund Sites 413 and 414, the Rocky Hill
           Municipal Well ("RHKW) site and the Montgomery Township
           Housing Development ("MTHD") site, both in Somerset County,
           New Jersey. Attached to these Comments, and incorporated
           herein by reference, are an Assessment of the RI/FS prepared
           by Geraghty ft Miller, Inc. ("GiM Assessment"), at well as a
           letter prepared by Roy P. We*ton, Inc. ("Waaton Letter*)
           addressing certain aspects of the RI.
                   On the basis of the record to date, including these
           Comments and attachments hereto, PCT recommends that the DEP
           adopt Alternative 2, Aquifer Isolation and Monitoring, for
           each of the two Superfund Sites. However, PGT earnestly
           draws attention to all its significant comments, both those
           set forth below and those presented in the two attachments.
                   PGT recognizes the great efforts invested by the DEP
           in the attempt to progress beyond the Remedial Action Master
           Plans ("RAMPs") for these two Superfund Sites which the U.S.
           Environmental Protection Agency ("USEPA") released in April
           1984 (MTHD) and August 1984 (RHKW) and upon which the USEPA
           and DEP based the Record of Decision ("ROD") of September 29,
           1987. Regrettably, however, the RZ/FS prepared by Woodward-
           Clyde Consultants ("WCC") it flawed and entirely inadequate
           to support the PRAP.
                   The RAMP for the MTHD (April 1984) indicated that "at
           present there is no reason to believe that the groundwater
           problems in Rocky Hill and Montgomery Township have been
           caused by different sources of contamination. The Rocky Hill
           Borough municipal well and the Montgomery Township homes with
           contaminated wells are about one-half mile apart.* (JACA
           RAMP at ES-2.) That hypothesis, born merely of proximity, is
           no longer tenable. The approach of the current WCC Remedial
           Investigation (*RX") — which tries to squeece all the
           diverse data by now collected into the mold of a single
           source of pollution — does not work. The RI ignores'and/or
           contradicts several aspects of the earlier RAMPs — without      ^
           explanation or, even, candid acknowledgment. The RI does not    5.
           face up to its own internal inconsistencies, which an
           objective review of the data now reveal. These internal          0
           inconsistencies negate the hypothesis of one, single source.      o
           Adherence to that hypothesis would guarantee aubjeetive,          ^
           inaccurate and arbitrary conclusions — and could lead only      „0
           to failure in implementing DEP'a environmental mission. The     <± ®
           DEP faces two distinct Superfund Sites, each with multiple      2. 5
           sources of TCE and other contaminants.

   IV V- 1. PCT did not contaminate the KTHD ground water. Xt is now
   *"' clear that PGT did not contaminate WTHD ground water at all.•
                   a. Hate of flow. The RI's own estimates of maximum
         rate of groundwater flow prove that PGT could not have
         contaminated ground water under the KTHD. PGT acquired its -
         property in 1972. TCE was'first discovered 7 years later, in
         1979, in residential wells throughout the MTHD.l' Even
         assuming the RI's maximum rate of flow (142 feet/year) and
         assuming (contrary to the data) that TCE contamination could
         flow northeastwards, in the direction of the MTHD, from PGT's
         property, and assuming (contrary to all available data) that
         a TCE spill occurred in 1972 and immediately reached ground
         water, the TCE could not have reached even one of the MTHD
         residential wells by 1979— or even to within 500 feet of
         the nearest such wellI (GfcM Assessment at 15 i Figure 10.)
         Yet : the group of MTHD wells most contaminated with TCE in
         1979 were thoses farthest awav from the PGT property, in
         Cleveland Circle. Even today, 16 years after PGT bought the
         property, a 1972 spill could not have reached such areas as
         Cleveland Circle even assuming a maximum rate of flow in the
         direction erroneously hypothesized. A 1979 or 1960 spill
         could not have reached any of the MTHD wells.
                   b. Direction of flow. Shallow ground water flows
         from the PGT property in a northwest for west-northwest)
         direction, under Route 206 — awav from the MTHD — and
         discharges near the Village Shopper to a tributary which
         flows north-northeastwards to Beden Brook. (G*M Assessment,
         Figures 1 and 7; pages 2, 11-12.) None of this shallow
         ground water could ever reach the MTHD residential wells.
         Available data indicate that deep ground water flow from the
         PGT property would be in the southeast direction — also away
           from the MTHD. (G&K Assessment at 3, 12.)
                   c. WCC's Inconsistent Selectivity in Using ECRA
           data. NCC's (and DEP's) reason for considering the PGT

           17. No proper investigation has been made of PRPs. The RX/FS
           failed to investigate FRFs adequately or rigorously. Past
           ownership and tenancy records were not sought. Land use,
           industrial and commercial activities were not rigorously
           investigated or documented. WCC seems to report casual,
           anecdotal information, at best.                           •

           that is the case, the TCE revealed by PCT'a ECRA monitoring

05/31/88 •   11*43   fiRENT FOX KINTNER FLOTK1N I W*N   NO.518   FWl4/8i5

         likely are present still contaminating the around water. The
         likely (and potential) sources of additional contamination
         would put more VOC into the groundwater at one end as
         counterpumping tries to remove VOC at the other. Expenditure
         of considerable resources on counterpumping is unlikely to
         yield any net environmental or health benefit.
         20* The public health rationale of the FS underlying the
         PRAP does not make sense, it rests on the non-existent
         public health risk if people in the KTHD continued to drink
         and bathe in contaminated water for a lifetime (of seventy
         years). Of course, that will not happen becaues the remedial
         actions already ordered by USEPA'require these homes no
         longer to use contaminated water, 'completely eliminat(ing]
         risk due to exposure to contaminated groundwater•• [USEPA
         ROD at 25".] Well sealing would avoid any careless
         reversions. Nonetheless, the PRAP proposes trying to clean
         out the volatile organic compounds (VOC) from the groundwater
         by pumping and evaporation into the air for seven years (or
         likely more) instead of letting nature do the job.
                        >
         21. VCC failed to find TCB sources. This failure is a
         glaring weakness of the RX. Not having any idea of the
         sources, WCC is in no position to design a rational FS,
         beyond the measures already mandated and undertaken plus
         Aquifer Isolation and Monitoring, Alternative 2.
         22. Precedents for Alternative 2. A review of relevant
         Superfund Records of Decisons ("RODS*) which address
         situations similar to the KTKO and RHKW contamination has
         identified at least three instances,!/ in which the USEPA
         determined that placing affected residences on municipal
         water, performing additional studies to identify sources,
         doing remediation work to eliminate the sources of the
         contamination, and allowing natural restoration of the ground
         water was sufficient to address the health and welfare
         concerni raised by regional aquifer contamination. These
         RODs appear to be the appropriate models for addressing the
         concern at the KTHD and the RHKW Superfund Sites,
         particularly since the installation of air stripping at the   .2
         RHMW in 1983 and the 1987 ROD for the HTHD have already         5
         addressed and eliminated any public health concerns.

     The Rl/FS identified an area on the north side of Route 518,
including land now owned or used by Prineeton Gamma-Tech, Inc. (PGT),
as a probable primary source of trichloroathene (TCE) .contamination.
2his identification appears to be principally influenced by shallow
ground-water quality data developed by PSI for an ECRX proceeding.
This report focuses primarily on a review and analysis of broader
regional data in regard to the two Suparfund Sites.

2.1 ftrmi** M»fr»y flow Conditions

          3ne study area is underlain by a consolidated bedrock
          aquifer system. Ctnplex fracture systems vithin the bedrock
          provide for the storage and transmission of ground vatar.
          According to the XL, the principal fracture set is
          reportedly vertical and along formation strike (North 65
          ——t).
          HO clear delineation can be developed between shallow and
          deeper ground-vatar flow and quality conditions 1on the basis
          of WCC's study because WCC's so called "deep* monitoring
          veils have open intervals over almost the entire thickness
          of the bedrock aquifer to the depth that the veils are
          completed. Although the "shallow" veils are really shallow,
          the so-called "deep" veils are, in truth, a shallow-deep
          combination.
          3ne regional shallow vater-level contour nap (Figure 6)
          developed by Ceraghty ( Killer, Inc. indicates that ground
          vatar in the upper portion of the Brunswick Aquifer is
          discharging to local stream tributaries, ttiis nap (based en
          HOC monitoring veils only) is of insufficient detail to
          allow for a determination of the position of the shallow
          ground-vater divide in the vicinity of the PSI sits.
          The site specific shallow vater-level contour nap for the
          PST site and vicinity (Figure 7) (developed by Geraghty i

3.2 CiuuriJ-Wa^gr nowPgnfljfrjflpff

     9ne study area is underlain by the Brunswick Formation, a consol-
idated bedrock aquifer unit. Ihis formation is overlain by a shallow
layer of weathered rock and residual soils derived from the Brunswick
Formation. Based on the field investigations conducted to data, the
xgT«rnr«t aquifer unit primarily occurs within the weathered portion
of the Brunswick Formation*

     in most consolidated bedrock aquifer systems, fractures and other
openings in the rock provide the principal avenues for the storage and
transmission of ground water. According to the XX, the principal
fracture set Is reportedly vertical and is along formation strike
(North 65° East) and tha bedrock units dip to the northwest at 12° to


     Previous work in the Brunswick Formation in Hew Jersey at major
punping centers has indicated that pumping frcn production wells in
the Brunswick Formation results in an anisotropie cons of depression
that is elliptical in shape and oriented along formation strike. As
discussed in the XX, "Ihe practical implication of the directional
hydraulic behavior of the Brunswick aquifer is that the ground water
is able to flow mars freely in the direction of strike, and therefore     3
the facility for contaminant migration would be greatest along            5
striks.* (Vol. 1, Page 9-C) 3his factor is important and win be dis-
cussed in sore detail later in the report.                                    £

to woe's study, two types of wells were installed; shallow monitoring     _

        o   No clear delineation can be developed between shallow and

            Well 2-D is completed to a depth of 150 feet and is open
            hole from 21 feet to 150 feet bgs; Monitoring WaU 11-D is
            completed to a depth of 250 feet and is open hole froa 25 to
            250 feet   bgs. Therefore, the so-called "deep monitoring
            veils11 provide for an assessment of ground-vater quality and
            flow conditions over almost the entire thickness of the
            bedrock aquifer system to the depth that the veils are
            completed and do not serve to differentiate between shallow
            and deep conditions. Table 1 provides a sunaary of
            construction details for the WOC monitoring vails.
        o    Indeed, the "deep" monitoring veils may provide conduits for
            the migration of shallow contamination to daepar portions of
            the bedrock aquifer. This migration is possible because of
      - v   the long open intervals in the "deep1* monitoring vails and

            clusters.

       da cancerNrisk associated vith A*™*] contact and inhalation of

     The non-carcinogenic risks associated vith metals and volatile
organic chemicals reported in ground-vater samples were also
recalculated. As stated in the HA, the hazard index is a ratio
between calculated dose intake (GDI) in the numerator to the reference
dose (RfD)in the denominator. The RfD represents the highest dosage
that vill not elicit a harmful effect. Hazard indices that exceed one
suggest that a harmful affect aight be observed in the population.

Montgomery Twp. & Rocky Hill (Somerset County)       Immediate release:
DEP TO HOLD MEETING JAN. 14                          December 16, 1985
ON WELL CONTAMINATION STUDY       .

          TRENTON—The state Department of Environmental Protection (DEP) will
hold "a public meeting Tuesday, Jan. 14, on the start of a Remedial Investigation/
Feasibility Study of contaminated well water at two Superfund sites in Somerset .
County — the Montgomery Township Housing Development and Rocky Hill Municipal
well field.                   •                -
          The meeting will be held at 7:30 p.m. by DEP's Hazardous Site Mitigation
Administration in the Montgomery Township Municipal Building, Route 206, in Belle Mead.
          Woodward Clyde Consultants of Wayne was awarded a $946,513 contract in
July and is expected to start field work for the study next month. The study will
determine the extent of contamination, whether the source is continuing and recommend
                x
remedial measures.     '
         A cooperative agreement between DEP and the U.S. Environmental Protection
Agency was signed in 1984 providing funds for the study which is expected to be
completed in the fall.                                                •
          The Montgomery Township Housing Development consists of 71 homes with
private wells of which about half had been found to have varying levels of                o
                                                                                          o
trichloroethylene (TCE). Although public water lines have been installed, some of
 he homeowners still have not hooked up to public water supply. In addition, the
 <ow of contaminants In the fractured rock aquifer Is difficult to predict and
                                         - •               " »            •   '"'
  ture samples may show contamination In previously clean wells.
          The Rocky Hill Municipal well field 1s a two-acre tract located south
                                                      \
   Washington Street and east of Route 206 which supplies-water for about 1,000
                                  (more)

===== SOURCE PDF FILE PAGE 195 (file index only; use the page number printed in the source text for evidence anchors) =====

 1EETING
,.dd 1                                             ;

residents. The well had been closed due to elevated levels of TCE and reopened in
1983 after the installation of water pollution control devices reduced the concen-
                                                                    •

trations. DEP has installed a monitoring well and is investigating the sources of
contamination and direction of groundwater flow.
           For further information contact DEP's Office of Community Relations at
(609) 984-3081.
